# Chapter 5: Source distribution weighted multisource domain adaptation without access to source data

Sk Miraj Ahmed[\*](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#cr0010); Dripta S. Raychaudhuri; Samet Oymak; Amit K. Roy-Chowdhury    ^()Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering, University of California Riverside, Riverside, CA, United States  
^∗ Corresponding author: email address: [sahme047@ucr.edu](mailto:sahme047@ucr.edu)

## Abstract

Unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) aims to learn a predictive model for an unlabeled domain by transferring knowledge from a separate labeled source domain. Conventional UDA approaches make the strong assumption of having access to the source data during training. This may not be very practical due to privacy, security, and storage concerns. A recent line of work addressed this problem and proposed an algorithm that transfers knowledge to the unlabeled target domain from a single-source model *without requiring access to the source data*. However, for adaptation purposes, if there are multiple trained source models available to choose from, this method has to go through adapting each and every model individually, to check for the best source. A better question to ask is the following: can we find the optimal combination of source models, with no source data and without target labels, whose performance is no worse than the single best source? The answer to this is given by a recent efficient algorithm ([Ahmed et al., 2021](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015)) which automatically combines the source models with suitable weights in such a way that it performs at least as good as the best source model. The work provided intuitive theoretical insights to justify their claim and extensive experiments were conducted on several benchmark datasets. In this chapter, we first review this work on multi-source source-free unsupervised domain adaptation followed by analysis of a new algorithm which we propose by relaxing some of the assumptions of this prior work. More specifically, instead of naively assuming source data distribution as uniform, we try to estimate it via a multilayer perceptron (MLP) in order to use this information for effective aggregation of source models.

### Keywords

Source free adaptation; Multi source adaptation; Unsupervised domain adaptation

##  1: Introduction

Deep neural networks have achieved proficiency in a multiple array of vision tasks ([He et al., 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0065); [Kirillov et al., 2019](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0100); [Long et al., 2015](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0135); [Redmon et al., 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0175)); however, these models have consistently fallen short in adapting to visual distributional shifts ([Luo et al., 2019](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0140)). Human recognition, on the other hand, is robust to such shifts, such as reading text in a new font or recognizing objects in unseen environments. Imparting such robustness toward distributional shifts to deep models is fundamental in applying these models to practical scenarios.

Unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) ([Ben-David et al., 2010](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0020); [Saenko et al., 2010](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0185)) seeks to bridge this performance gap due to domain shift via adaptation of the model on small amounts of unsupervised data from the target domain. The majority of current approaches ([Ganin et al., 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0050); [Hoffman et al., 2018b](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0080)) optimize a two-fold objective: (i) minimize the empirical risk on the source data and (ii) make the target and source features indistinguishable from each other. Minimizing distribution divergence between domains by matching the distribution statistical moments at different orders has also been explored extensively ([Peng et al., 2019](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0165); [Sun et al., 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0205)).

A shortcoming of all the above approaches is the transductive scenario in which they operate, i.e., the source data is required for adaptation purposes. In a real-world setting, source data may not be available for a variety of reasons. Privacy and security are the primary concern, with the data possibly containing sensitive information. Another crucial reason is storage issues, i.e., source datasets may contain videos or high-resolution images and it might not be practical to transfer or store on different platforms. Consequently, it is imperative to develop unsupervised adaptation approaches which can adapt the source models to the target domain *without access to the source data* ([Fig. 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#f0010)).

![Fig. 1 Problem setup. Standard unsupervised multisource domain adaptation (UDA) utilizes the source data, along with the models trained on the source, to perform adaptation on a target domain. In contrast, Ahmed et al. (2021) introduced a setting which adapts multiple models without requiring access to the source data.](images/S0169716122000566/f05-01-9780443184307.jpg)

[Fig. 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-f0010) Problem setup. Standard unsupervised multisource domain adaptation (UDA) utilizes the source data, along with the models trained on the source, to perform adaptation on a target domain. In contrast, [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015) introduced a setting which adapts multiple models without requiring access to the source data.

Recent works ([Li et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0120); [Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125)) attempt this by adapting a single-source model to a target domain without accessing the source data. However, an underlying assumption of these methods is that the most correlated source model is provided by an oracle for adaptation purposes. A more challenging and practical scenario entails adaptation from a *bag of source models*—each of these source domains is correlated to the target by different amounts and adaptation involves not only incorporating the combined prior knowledge from multiple models, but simultaneously preventing the possibility of negative transfer. In this chapter, we first discuss the problem of unsupervised *multisource adaptation without access to source data* proposed by [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015), followed by analysis of a new algorithm which we design based on less restrictive assumption compared to [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015). The proposed algorithm is based on the principles of pseudo-labeling and information maximization and provide intuitive theoretical insights to show that this framework guarantees performance better than the best available source and minimize the effect of negative transfer.

To solve this problem of multiple source model adaptation without accessing the source data, we deploy *Information Maximization (IM)* loss ([Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125)) on the weighted combination of target soft labels from all the source models. We also use the pseudo-label strategy inspired from deep cluster method ([Caron et al., 2018](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0030)), along with the IM loss to minimize noisy cluster assignment of the features. Unlike [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015), which assigns fixed weight to each of the source models, irrespective of the target samples, the new proposed optimization assigns different source weights to each of the target samples based on estimated source distributions. Since we do not have access to the source data, our framework utilizes multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) to estimate these distributions in order to minimize empirical target risk. Closely related to [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015), this framework jointly adapts the feature encoders from sources as well as the corresponding source weights (generated by passing target samples through the MLPs), combining which the target model is obtained.

###  1.1: Main contributions

We address the problem of multiple source UDA, with no access to the source data. Toward solving the problem, we make the following contributions:

*   • We propose a novel UDA algorithm which operates without requiring access to the source data. We do so by training some MLPs in order to estimate source distributions which efficiently help model the target distribution under a stronger theoretically sound combination rule. We term it as source distribution (estimated by MLPs) weighted Data free multisourCE unsupervISed domain adaptatiON (DECISION-mlp). Our algorithm automatically identifies the optimal blend of source models (which is also a function of target samples) to generate the target model by optimizing a carefully designed unsupervised loss.
*   • Under intuitive assumptions, we establish theoretical guarantees on the performance of the target model which shows that it is consistently at least as good as deploying the single best source model, thus, minimizing negative transfer.
*   • We validate our claim by numerical experiments, demonstrating the practical benefits of our approach.

##  2: Related works

In this section, we present a brief overview of the literature in the area of UDA in both the single and multiple sources scenario, as well as the closely related setting of hypothesis transfer learning.

###  2.1: Unsupervised domain adaptation

UDA methods have been used for a variety of tasks, including image classification ([Tzeng et al., 2017](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0215)), semantic segmentation ([Paul et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0160)), and object detection ([Hsu et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0085)). Besides the feature space adaptation methods based on the paradigms of moment matching ([Peng et al., 2019](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0165); [Sun et al., 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0205)) and adversarial learning ([Ganin et al., 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0050); [Tzeng et al., 2017](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0215)), recent works have explored pixel space adaptation via image translation ([Hoffman et al., 2018b](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0080)). All existing UDA methods require access to labeled source data, which may not be available in many applications.

###  2.2: Hypothesis transfer learning

Similar to our objective, hypothesis transfer learning (HTL) ([Ahmed et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0010); [Perrot and Habrard, 2015](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0170); [Singh et al., 2018](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0200)) aims to transfer learnt source hypotheses to a target domain without access to source data. However, data is assumed to be labeled in the target domain in contrast to our scenario, limiting its applicability to real-world settings. Recently, [Li et al. (2020)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0120) and [Liang et al. (2020)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125) extend the standard HTL setting to unsupervised target data (U-HTL) by adapting single-source hypotheses via pseudo-labeling. Our paper takes this one step further by introducing multiple source models, which may or may not be positively correlated with the target domain.

###  2.3: Multisource domain adaptation

Multisource domain adaptation (MSDA) extends the standard UDA setting by incorporating knowledge from multiple source models. Latent space transformation methods ([Zhao et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0245)) aim to align the features of different domains by optimizing a discrepancy measure or an adversarial loss. Discrepancy based methods seek to align the domains by minimizing measures such as maximum mean discrepancy ([Guo et al., 2018](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0060); [Zhao et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0245)) and Rényi-divergence ([Hoffman et al., 2018a](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0075)). Adversarial methods aim to make features from multiple domains indistinguishable to a domain discriminator by optimizing GAN loss ([Xu et al., 2018](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0230)), ℋ![si5\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si5_e.png)\-divergence ([Zhao et al., 2018](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0240)), and Wasserstein distance ([Li et al., 2018](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0115); [Wang et al., 2019](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0225)). Domain generative methods ([Lin et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0130); [Russo et al., 2019](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0180)) use some form of domain translation, such as the CycleGAN ([Zhu et al., 2017](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0250)), to perform adaptation at the pixel level. All these methods assume access to the source data during adaptation.

###  2.4: Source-free multisource UDA

This line of work is very recent and first proposed and solved in [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015). Transferring knowledge from multiple source models to an unlabeled target domain by optimizing suitable source weights is the key idea of this paper, which we discuss in detail in the next section. Inspired by this work, some very recent works also address this problem. [Shen et al. (2022)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0195) show the benefits of selective pseudo-labeling on target domain data from theoretical and empirical perspectives, whereas [Dong et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0040) design a confident-anchor-induced pseudo label generator to mine confident pseudo-labels for the target domain in a multisource-free setting. Closely related to these works, we focus on a novel model aggregation scheme which is generic enough to be used on top of any existing methods for improved performance. In this chapter, we only analyze the effect of this model aggregation scheme on the algorithm in [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015).

##  3: Problem setting

We address the problem of jointly adapting multiple models, trained on a variety of domains, to a new target domain with access to only samples without annotations from the target. In this work, we will be considering the adaptation of classification models with *K* categories and the input space being 𝒳![si6\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si6_e.png). Formally, let us consider we have a set of source models {*θ*[*S*]^(*j*)}[*j* = 1]^(*n*)![si7\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si7_e.png), where the *j*^(*th*) model *θ*[*S*]^(*j*):𝒳→ℝ^(*K*)![si8\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si8_e.png) is a classification model learned using the source dataset 𝒟[*S*]^(*j*) = {*x*[*S*[*j*]]^(*i*), *y*[*S*[*j*]]^(*i*)}[*i* = 1]^(*N*[*j*])![si9\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si9_e.png), with *N*[*j*] data points, where *x*[*S*[*j*]]^(*i*)![si10\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si10_e.png) and *y*[*S*[*j*]]^(*i*)![si11\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si11_e.png) denote the *i*th source image and the corresponding label, respectively. Now, given a target unlabeled dataset 𝒟[*T*] = {*x*[*T*]^(*i*)}[*i* = 1]^(*N*[*T*])![si12\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si12_e.png), the problem is to learn a classification model *θ*[*T*]:𝒳→ℝ^(*K*)![si13\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si13_e.png), using only the learned source models, without any access to the source datasets. Note that this is different from most of the multisource domain adaptation methods in literature, which also utilize the source data while learning the target model *θ*[*T*]. Since our proposed framework is built upon the first work on source-free multisource UDA (DECISION) ([Ahmed et al., 2021](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015)), we will first discuss DECISION in detail and then move on to the analysis of the modification we made.

 

##  4: Practical motivation

To understand the practicality of this specific problem setting, we provide example of some real applications. For example, let's say we want to classify some images in the target which belong to the domain *sketches*. We also have access to the trained source models which are trained on domains like *Art*, *Clipart*, *Product*, *Real*, etc. Images from *Art* domain are mostly visually and semantically similar to *sketches* domain compared to other sources. So, intuitively most images from the target domain should acquire highest adaptation performance from the *Art* domain. However, in practical scenarios these source images are not available and as a result there is no way of computing similarities between source and target images directly, which are crucial for computing weights for the corresponding sources given the target image. In this situation, this problem setting comes into play where the target images are adapted by the source models without accessing the source data. During adaptation, each of the target images should accordingly weight the source models based on just the source model parameters.

##  5: Overall framework of DECISION ([Ahmed et al., 2021](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015))&#x2014;A review

Each of the source models can be decomposed into two modules: the feature extractor *ϕ*[*S*]^(*i*):𝒳→ℝ^(*d*[*i*])![si14\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si14_e.png) and the classifier *ψ*[*S*]^(*i*):ℝ^(*d*[*i*])→ℝ^(*K*)![si15\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si15_e.png). Here, *d*[*i*] refers to the feature dimension of the *i*th model, while *K* refers to the number of categories. The overall aim is to estimate the target model *θ*[*T*] ([Fig. 2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#f0015)) by combining knowledge only from the given source models in a manner that automatically rejects poor source models, i.e., those which are irrelevant for the target domain.

![Fig. 2 Overall framework of DECISION (Ahmed et al., 2021): Final classification layers of all the sources are frozen and the source feature encoders are jointly optimized along with their corresponding weights to get the target predictor by combining those.](images/S0169716122000566/f05-02-9780443184307.jpg)

[Fig. 2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-f0015) Overall framework of DECISION ([Ahmed et al., 2021](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015)): Final classification layers of all the sources are frozen and the source feature encoders are jointly optimized along with their corresponding weights to get the target predictor by combining those.

At the core of this framework lies a model aggregation scheme ([Hoffman et al., 2018a](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0075); [Mansour et al., 2009](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0145)), wherein the key idea is to learn a set of weights {*α*[*i*]}[*i* = 1]^(*n*)![si16\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si16_e.png) corresponding to each of the source models such that *α*[*k*] ≥ 0 and $\\sum\_{k = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{k} = 1$![si17\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si17_e.png). These weights represent a probability mass function over the source domains, with a higher value implying higher transferability from that particular domain, and are used to combine the source hypotheses accordingly. However, unlike previous works, this work jointly adapts each individual model and simultaneously learn these weights by utilizing solely the unlabeled target instances. In what follows, we describe our training strategy used to achieve this in detail.

###  5.1: Weighted information maximization

Since one does not have access to the labeled source or target data, the authors propose to fix the source classifiers, {*ψ*[*S*]^(*i*)}[*i* = 1]^(*n*)![si18\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si18_e.png), since it contains the class distribution information of the source domain and adapted solely the feature maps {*ϕ*[*S*]^(*i*)}[*i* = 1]^(*n*)![si19\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si19_e.png) via the principle of information maximization ([Bridle et al., 1992](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0025); [Krause et al., 2010](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0105); [Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125); [Oymak and Gulcu, 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0155)). The motivation behind the adaptation process stems from the *cluster assumption* ([Chapelle et al., 2009](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0035)) in semisupervised learning, which hypothesizes that the discriminative models’ decision boundaries should be located in regions of the input space which are not densely populated. To achieve this, the authors minimize a conditional entropy term (i.e., for a given input example) ([Grandvalet and Bengio, 2005](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0055)) as follows:

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{ent}} = - \\mathbb{E}\_{x\_{T} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\left\\lbrack {\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{K}\\delta\_{j}(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}))\\log(\\delta\_{j}(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T})))} \\right\\rbrack} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si20\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si20_e.png)  (1)

where $\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}) = \\sum\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j}\\theta\_{S}^{j}(x\_{T})$![si21\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si21_e.png), and *δ*(⋅) denotes the softmax operation with $\\delta\_{j}(v) = \\frac{e^{v\_{j}}}{\\sum\_{i = 1}^{K}e^{v\_{i}}}$![si22\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si22_e.png) for *v* ∈ ℝ^(*K*)![si23\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si23_e.png). Intuitively, if a source *θ*[*S*]^(*j*)![si24\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si24_e.png) has good transferability on the target and consequently has smaller value of the conditional entropy, optimizing the term ([1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#fo0010)) over {*θ*[*S*]^(*j*),*α*[*j*]}![si25\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si25_e.png) will result in higher value of *α*[*j*] than rest of the weights.

While entropy minimization effectively captures the cluster assumption when training with partial labels, in an unsupervised setting, it may lead to degenerate solutions, such as always predicting a single class in an attempt to minimize conditional entropy. To control such degenerate solutions, the authors incorporate the idea of class diversity: configurations in which class labels are assigned evenly across the dataset are preferred. A simple way to encode the preference toward class balance is to maximize the entropy of the empirical label distribution ([Bridle et al., 1992](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0025)) as follows,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{div}} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{K} - {\\overset{-}{p}}\_{j}\\log{\\overset{-}{p}}\_{j}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si26\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si26_e.png)  (2)

where $\\overset{-}{p} = \\mathbb{E}\_{x\_{T} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\lbrack\\delta(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}))\\rbrack$![si27\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si27_e.png). Combining the terms ([1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#fo0010)) and ([2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#fo0015)), we arrive at

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{IM}} = \\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{div}} - \\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{ent}}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si28\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si28_e.png)  (3)

which is the empirical estimate of the mutual information between the target data and the labels under the aggregate model *θ*[*T*]. Although maximizing this loss makes the predictions on the target data more confident and globally diverse, it may sometime still fail to restrict erroneous label assignment. Inspired by [Liang et al. (2020)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125), the authors propose a pseudo-labeling strategy in an effort to contain this mislabeling.

###  5.2: Weighted pseudo-labeling

As a result of domain shift, information maximization may result in some instances being clubbed with the wrong class cluster. These wrong predictions get reinforced over the course of training and lead to a phenomenon termed as *confirmation bias* ([Tarvainen and Valpola, 2017](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0210)). Aiming to contain this effect, the authors adopt a self-supervised clustering strategy ([Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125)) inspired from the DeepCluster technique ([Caron et al., 2018](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0030)).

First, calculation the cluster centroids induced by each source model for the whole target dataset is done as follows:

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mu\_{k\_{j}}^{(0)} = \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{x\_{T} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\delta\_{k}({\\widehat{\\theta}}\_{S}^{j}(x\_{T})){\\widehat{\\phi}}\_{S}^{j}(x\_{T})}{\\sum\\limits\_{x\_{T} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\delta\_{k}({\\widehat{\\theta}}\_{S}^{j}(x\_{T}))}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si29\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si29_e.png)  (4)

where the cluster centroid of class *k* obtained from source *j* at iteration *i* is denoted as *μ*[*k*[*j*]]^((*i*))![si30\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si30_e.png), and *θ̂*[*S*]^(*j*) = (*ψ*[*S*]^(*j*) ∘ *ϕ̂*[*S*]^(*j*))![si31\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si31_e.png) denotes the source from the previous iteration. Assuming feature dimensions are same across all sources, these source-specific centroids are combined in accordance with the current aggregation weights on each source model as follows,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mu\_{k}^{(0)} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j}\\mu\_{k\_{j}}^{(0)}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si32\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si32_e.png)  (5)

Next, computation of the pseudo-label of each sample is done by assigning it to its nearest cluster centroid in the feature space,

$\\begin{array}{r} {ŷ\_{T}^{(0)} = \\text{arg}\\;\\underset{k}{\\text{min}}\\; \\parallel {\\widehat{\\phi}}\_{T}(x\_{T}) - \\mu\_{k}^{(0)} \\parallel\_{2}^{2}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si33\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si33_e.png)  (6)

where ${\\widehat{\\phi}}\_{T}(x\_{T}) = \\sum\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j}{\\widehat{\\phi}}\_{S}^{j}(x\_{T})$![si34\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si34_e.png). Next, reiteration of this process is done to get the updated centroids and pseudo-labels as follows,

$\\begin{aligned} \\mu\_{k\_{j}}^{(1)} & {= \\frac{\\sum\\limits\_{x\_{T} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}1\\{ ŷ\_{T}^{(0)} = k\\}{\\widehat{\\phi}}\_{S}^{j}(x\_{T})}{\\sum\\limits\_{x\_{T} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}1(\\widehat{y^{t\_{0}}} = k)}} \\\\ \\end{aligned}$

![si35\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si35_e.png)  (7)

$\\begin{aligned} \\mu\_{k}^{(1)} & {= \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j}\\mu\_{k\_{j}}^{(1)}} \\\\ \\end{aligned}$

![si36\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si36_e.png)  (8)

$\\begin{aligned} ŷ\_{T}^{(1)} & {= \\text{arg}\\;\\underset{k}{\\text{min}}\\; \\parallel {\\widehat{\\phi}}\_{T}(x\_{T}) - \\mu\_{k}^{(1)} \\parallel\_{2}^{2}} \\\\ \\end{aligned}$

![si37\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si37_e.png)  (9)

where 1![si38\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si38_e.png)(⋅) is an indicator function which gives a value of 1 when the argument is true. While this alternating process of computing cluster centroids and pseudo-labeling can be repeated multiple times to get stationary pseudo-labels, one round is sufficient for all practical purposes. One can then obtain the cross-entropy loss w.r.t. these pseudo-labels as follows:

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{pl}}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{T}) = - \\mathbb{E}\_{x\_{T} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{K}1\\{ ŷ\_{T} = k\\}\\log\\delta\_{k}(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T})).} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si39\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si39_e.png)  (10)

Note that the pseudo-labels are updated regularly after a certain number of iterations as discussed in [Section 9](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#s0095).

###  5.3: Optimization

In summary, given *n* source hypothesis {*θ*[*S*]^(*j*)}[*j* = 1]^(*n*) = {*ψ*[*S*]^(*j*) ∘ *ϕ*[*S*]^(*j*)}[*j* = 1]^(*n*)![si40\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si40_e.png) and target data 𝒟[*T*] = {*x*[*T*]^(*i*)}[*i* = 1]^(*n*[*T*])![si41\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si41_e.png), the classifier from each of the sources is fixed and optimization over the parameters of {*ϕ*[*S*]^(*j*)}[*j* = 1]^(*n*)![si42\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si42_e.png) and the aggregation weights {*α*[*j*]}[*j* = 1]^(*n*)![si43\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si43_e.png) are done jointly in an end-to-end manner. The final objective is given by,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{tot} = \\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{ent}} - \\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{div}} + \\lambda\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{pl}}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si44\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si44_e.png)  (11)

The above objective is used to solve the following optimization problem,

$\\begin{array}{ll} {\\operatorname{minimize}\\limits\_{{\\{\\phi\_{S}^{j}\\}}\_{j = 1}^{n},{\\{\\alpha\_{j}\\}}\_{j = 1}^{n}}\\quad} & \\mathcal{L}\_{tot} \\\\ {\\text{subject\\ to}\\quad} & {\\alpha\_{j} \\geq 0,\\forall j \\in \\{ 1,2,\\ldots,n\\},} \\\\ \\quad & {\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j} = 1} \\\\ \\quad & \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si45\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si45_e.png)  (12)

After obtaining the optimal set of *ϕ*[*S*]^(*j*\*)![si46\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si46_e.png) and *α*[*j*]^(\*)![si47\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si47_e.png), the optimal target hypothesis is computed as $\\theta\_{T} = \\sum\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j}^{\*}(\\psi\_{S}^{j} \\circ \\phi\_{S}^{j\*})$![si48\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si48_e.png). To solve the optimization ([12](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#fo0065)), steps of [Algorithm 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#b0010) are followed as stated below.

[Algorithm 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#cf0410)

Overview of DECISION.

![](images/S0169716122000566/u05-01-9780443184307.jpg) 

##  6: Theoretical insights

###  6.1: Theoretical motivation behind DECISION

DECISION aims to find the optimal weights {*α*[*j*]}[*j* = 1]^(*n*)![si43\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si43_e.png) for each source and takes a convex combination of the source predictors to obtain the target predictor. It shows that under intuitive assumptions on the source and target distributions, there exists a simple choice of target predictor, which can perform better than or equal to the best source model being applied directly on the target data.

Following the notations in [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015), *L* is the loss function which maps the pair of model-predicted label and the ground-truth label to a scalar. Expected loss over *k*th source distribution is denoted by *Q*[*S*]^(*k*)![si50\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si50_e.png) using the source predictor *θ* via ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*k*), *θ*) = 𝔼[*x*]\[*L*(*θ*(*x*), *y*)\] = ∫[*x*]*L*(*θ*(*x*), *y*)*Q*[*S*]^(*k*)(*x*)*d**x*![si51\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si51_e.png). *θ*[*S*]^(*k*)![si52\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si52_e.png) is the optimal source predictor given by $\\theta\_{S}^{k} = \\text{arg}\\;\\underset{\\theta}{\\text{min}}\\;\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{S}^{k},\\theta)\\;\\forall\\; 1 \\leq k \\leq n$![si53\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si53_e.png). Based on the assumption in [Ahmed et al. (2021)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0015), the target distribution is in the span of source distributions, which can be formalized by expressing the target distribution as an affine combination of source distributions, i.e., $Q\_{T}(x) = \\sum\_{k = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{k}Q\_{S}^{k}(x):\\lambda\_{k} \\geq 0,\\sum\_{k = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{k} = 1$![si54\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si54_e.png). Under this assumption, the target predictor can be expressed as $\\theta\_{T}(x) = \\sum\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{k}Q\_{S}^{k}(x)}{\\sum\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}Q\_{S}^{j}(x)}\\theta\_{S}^{k}(x)$![si55\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si55_e.png), then the theoretical claim is established as stated in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010).

Lemma 1.

Assume that the loss *L*(*θ*(*x*), *y*) is convex in its first argument and that there exists a *λ* ∈ ℝ^(*n*)![si56\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si56_e.png) where *λ* ≥ 0![si57\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si57_e.png) and *λ*^⊤1![si38\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si38_e.png) = 1![si59\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si59_e.png) such that the target distribution is exactly equal to the mixture of source distributions, i.e., $Q\_{T} = \\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{i}Q\_{S}^{i}$![si60\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si60_e.png). Set the target predictor as the following convex combination of the optimal source predictors

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\theta\_{T}(x) = \\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{k}Q\_{S}^{k}(x)}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}Q\_{S}^{j}(x))}\\theta\_{S}^{k}(x).} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si61\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si61_e.png)

Recall the pseudo-labeling loss ([10](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#fo0055)). Then, for this target predictor, over the target distribution, the unsupervised loss induced by the pseudo-labels and the supervised loss are both less than or equal to the loss induced by the best source predictor. In particular,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{T}) \\leq \\min\\limits\_{1 \\leq j \\leq n}\\;\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{S}^{j}).} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si62\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si62_e.png)

Let *α* = arg min[1 ≤ *j* ≤ *n*] ℒ(*Q*[*T*], *θ*[*S*]^(*j*))![si63\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si63_e.png). Additionally, this inequality is strict if the entries of *λ* are strictly positive and there exists a source *i* for which the strict inequality ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*i*)) < ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*α*))![si64\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si64_e.png) holds.

Before going into the proof, we will discuss how does this lemma translates to DECISION and how it motivated us to analyze DECISION-mlp.

The key difference between DECISION and the predictor in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010) is the algorithm's combination rule, which fine-tunes the feature extractors of each source model unlike [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010). However, each source has an individual weight which is agnostic to the target data, whereas [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010) uses different weights per input instance. The intuitive justification for choosing this input-agnostic weighting strategy is provided below.

Since one does not know the information about the source distributions (due to the unavailability of source data), a good choice is to consider the least informative of all the distributions, i.e., uniform distribution for sources by the *Principle of Maximum Entropy* ([Jaynes, 1957](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0095)). This uniformity is assumed over the target support set 𝒳![si6\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si6_e.png). In what follows, the algorithm considers the restrictions of the source distributions to the target support 𝒳![si6\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si6_e.png). Mathematically, the assumption is *Q*[*S*]^(*k*)(*x*) = *c*[*k*]𝒰(*x*)![si67\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si67_e.png) when restricted to the support set *x* ∈ 𝒳![si68\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si68_e.png), where *c*[*k*] is a scaling factor which captures the relative contribution of source *k* and 𝒰(*x*)![si69\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si69_e.png) has value 1. Plugging this value of the distribution in the combination rule in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010), we get $\\theta\_{T}(x) = \\sum\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{k}c\_{k}}{\\sum\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}c\_{j}}\\theta\_{S}^{k}(x)$![si70\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si70_e.png) by the following steps:

$\\begin{array}{ll} {\\theta\_{T}\\left( x \\right)} & {= \\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{k}Q\_{S}^{k}\\left( x \\right)}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}Q\_{S}^{j}\\left( x \\right)}\\theta\_{S}^{k}\\left( x \\right)} \\\\ & {= \\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{k}c\_{k}\\mathcal{U}\\left( x \\right)}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}c\_{j}\\mathcal{U}\\left( x \\right)}\\theta\_{S}^{k}\\left( x \\right)} \\\\ & {= \\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{k}c\_{k}}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}c\_{j}}\\theta\_{S}^{k}\\left( x \\right)} \\\\ & \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si71\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si71_e.png)  (13)

This term consisting of *λ*[*k*] and *c*[*k*] essentially becomes the weighting term *α*[*k*] in DECISION. The next step is to put this value of *θ*[*T*] to solve the optimization ([12](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#fo0065)) jointly with respect to this *α*[*k*] and *ϕ*[*S*]^(*k*)![si72\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si72_e.png). Thus, DECISION will return us a favorable combination of source hypotheses, satisfying the bounds in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010), under the uniformity assumption of source distributions. However, without assumption of source uniformity one can try to estimate the *Q*[*S*]^(*k*)![si50\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si50_e.png)s in order to minimize target risk, which we will discuss in the next section as the proposed framework DECISION-mlp ([Fig. 3](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#f0020)).

![Fig. 3 Weight calculation in modified framework with MLP: Instead of setting α’s as free trainable parameters, it can be expressed as a function of source distributions via multilayer perceptrons (MLPs). For the end-to-end training source feature encoders as well as the MLPs are trained jointly.](images/S0169716122000566/f05-03-9780443184307.jpg)

[Fig. 3](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-f0020) Weight calculation in modified framework with MLP: Instead of setting *α*’s as free trainable parameters, it can be expressed as a function of source distributions via multilayer perceptrons (MLPs). For the end-to-end training source feature encoders as well as the MLPs are trained jointly.

 

##  7: Source distribution dependent weights (DECISION-mlp)

The mixing weights in the model aggregation scheme in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010), which also stems from [Mansour et al. (2009)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0145) and [Hoffman et al. (2018a)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0075), are functions of the source distributions and the target samples. Due to absence of source data, assumption of uniform source distribution leads to [Algorithm 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#b0010). However, without assuming anything about source distribution it is possible to estimate it via MLPs. Let's assume there are *n* MLPs ℳ[*S*]^(*k*) ∀ *k* = 1, 2, …*n*![si74\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si74_e.png) to estimate *n* source distributions [(Fig. 3)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#f0020). Target features are obtained by passing each and every sample through the feature encoders and concatenate them to get a vector of size *d* where $d = \\sum\_{i = 1}^{n}d\_{i}$![si75\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si75_e.png). If the target sample is *x*[*T*]^(*i*)![si76\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si76_e.png), then the concatenated vector *ϕ*(*x*[*T*]^(*i*))![si77\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si77_e.png) can be expressed as *ϕ*(*X*[*T*]^(*i*)) = \[*ϕ*[*S*]¹(*x*[*T*]^(*i*)), *ϕ*[*S*]²(*x*[*T*]^(*i*)), …*ϕ*[*S*]^(*n*)(*x*[*T*]^(*i*))\]^⊤ ∈ ℝ^(*d*)![si78\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si78_e.png). Next *ϕ*(*x*[*T*]^(*i*))![si77\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si77_e.png) is fed through all the MLPs to get *n* scalars {ℳ[*S*]^(*k*)(*ϕ*(*X*[*T*]^(*i*)))}[*k* = 1]^(*n*)![si80\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si80_e.png), which are analogous to *λ*[*k*]*Q*[*S*]^(*k*)(*x*)![si81\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si81_e.png)’s in the expression of the target predictor in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010). Clearly these scalars, upon normalizing emulate *α*’s as a function of both source distributions and target samples. In short, if the *i*th sample has a weight *α*[*j*]^(*i*)![si82\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si82_e.png) corresponding to *j*th source, then

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\alpha\_{j}^{i} = \\frac{\\mathcal{M}\_{S}^{j}(\\phi(x\_{T}^{i}))}{\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\mathcal{M}\_{S}^{k}(\\phi(x\_{T}^{i}))}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si83\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si83_e.png)  (14)

In this new setting, the loss functions in our objective are modified slightly. Let us denote the modified entropy, diversity, and pseudo-label losses as ℒ[ent]^′![si84\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si84_e.png), ℒ[div]^′![si85\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si85_e.png), and ℒ[pl]^′![si86\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si86_e.png), respectively. Then,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{ent}}^{\\prime} = - \\mathbb{E}\_{x\_{T}^{i} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\left\\lbrack {\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{K}\\delta\_{j}(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}^{i}))\\log(\\delta\_{j}(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}^{i})))} \\right\\rbrack} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si87\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si87_e.png)  (15)

where $\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}^{i}) = \\sum\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j}^{i}\\theta\_{S}^{j}(x\_{T}^{i})$![si88\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si88_e.png).

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{div}}^{\\prime} = \\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{K} - {\\overset{-}{p}}\_{j}^{\\prime}\\log{\\overset{-}{p}}\_{j}^{\\prime}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si89\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si89_e.png)  (16)

where $\\overset{-}{p} = \\mathbb{E}\_{x\_{T}^{i} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\lbrack\\delta(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}^{i}))\\rbrack$![si90\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si90_e.png) and,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{pl}}^{\\prime}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{T}) = - \\mathbb{E}\_{x\_{T}^{i} \\in \\mathcal{D}\_{T}}\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{K}1\\{ ŷ\_{T}^{\\prime} = k\\}\\log\\delta\_{k}(\\theta\_{T}(x\_{T}^{i})).} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si91\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si91_e.png)  (17)

where *ŷ*[*T*]^′![si92\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si92_e.png) is calculated in the same manner as *ŷ*[*T*]![si93\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si93_e.png) but with the modified MLP weights.

The overall modified objective function in this scenario becomes,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}\_{tot}^{\\prime} = \\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{ent}}^{\\prime} - \\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{div}}^{\\prime} + \\lambda\\mathcal{L}\_{\\text{pl}}^{\\prime}} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si94\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si94_e.png)  (18)

 

which gives rise to a new optimization problem as follows:

$\\begin{array}{ll} {\\operatorname{minimize}\\limits\_{{\\{\\phi\_{S}^{j}\\}}\_{j = 1}^{n},{\\{\\mathcal{M}\_{S}^{j}\\}}\_{j = 1}^{n}}\\quad} & \\mathcal{L}\_{tot}^{\\prime} \\\\ {\\text{subject\\ to}\\quad} & {\\alpha\_{j}^{i} = \\frac{\\mathcal{M}\_{S}^{j}(\\phi(x\_{T}^{i}))}{\\sum\\limits\_{k = 1}^{n}\\mathcal{M}\_{S}^{k}(\\phi(x\_{T}^{i}))},} \\\\ \\quad & {\\alpha\_{j}^{i} \\geq 0,} \\\\ \\quad & {\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\alpha\_{j}^{i} = 1,\\forall j \\in \\{ 1,2,\\ldots,n\\},\\forall i \\in \\{ 1,2,\\ldots,n\_{T}\\}} \\\\ \\quad & \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si95\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si95_e.png)  (19)

To solve this optimization problem, we follow the steps of [Algorithm 2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#b0015). We name the algorithm as DECISION-mlp which is a variant of DECISION. Note that, in [Algorithm 2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#b0015) we pass batch of target samples instead of a single sample to the MLPs. This is done in order to reduce computational complexity. Essentially the loss functions above are computed with respect to target samples of batch size one and can be extended easily to a batch size greater than one.

[Algorithm 2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#cf0500)

Overview of DECISION-mlp.

![](images/S0169716122000566/u05-02-9780443184307.jpg) 

##  8: Proof of [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010)

[Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010) holds true for both DECISION and DECISION-mlp, the former being a special case and the latter being the more general one, since DECISION-mlp is designed so as to mimic [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010) completely without any assumption on the source distributions. Now we prove [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010) as follows,

Proof

We can see that the left hand-side of the inequality in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010) can be upper-bounded by some loss as follows,

$\\begin{array}{cl} {\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{T}) = \\int\_{x}Q\_{T}(x)L(\\theta\_{T}(x),y) =} & {\\int\_{x}Q\_{T}(x)L\\left( {\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{i}Q\_{S}^{i}(x)}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}Q\_{S}^{j}(x))}\\theta\_{S}^{i}(x),y} \\right)dx} \\\\ & \\text{(Jensen's\\ inequality)} \\\\ & {\\leq \\int\_{x}Q\_{T}(x)\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{i}Q\_{S}^{i}(x)}{\\sum\\limits\_{j = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{j}Q\_{S}^{j}(x))}L(\\theta\_{S}^{i}(x),y)dx\\;\\;} \\\\ & \\text{(distribution\\ assumption)} \\\\ & {= \\int\_{x}Q\_{T}(x)\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\frac{\\lambda\_{i}Q\_{S}^{i}(x)}{Q\_{T}(x)}L(\\theta\_{S}^{i}(x),y)dx\\;\\;\\;} \\\\ & \\text{(changing\\ the\\ order\\ of\\ summation)} \\\\ & {= \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{i}\\int\_{x}Q\_{S}^{i}(x)L(\\theta\_{S}^{i}(x),y)dx\\;\\;} \\\\ & {= \\sum\\limits\_{i}\\lambda\_{i}\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{S}^{i}(x),\\theta\_{S}^{i})} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si96\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si96_e.png)  (20)

Now for the R.H.S. we can write this loss as follows,

$\\begin{array}{cl} {\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{S}^{j})} & {= \\int\_{x}Q\_{T}(x)L(\\theta\_{S}^{j}(x),y)dx} \\\\ & {= \\int\_{x}\\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{i}Q\_{S}^{i}(x)L(\\theta\_{S}^{j}(x),y)dx} \\\\ & {= \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{i}\\int\_{x}Q\_{S}^{i}L(\\theta\_{S}^{j}(x),y)dx} \\\\ & {= \\sum\\limits\_{i = 1}^{n}\\lambda\_{i}\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{S}^{i}(x),\\theta\_{S}^{j})} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si97\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si97_e.png)  (21)

Now recall that,

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\theta\_{S}^{k} = \\arg\\min\\limits\_{\\theta}\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{S}^{k},\\theta)\\quad\\text{for}\\quad 1 \\leq k \\leq n.} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si98\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si98_e.png)

 

This means *θ*[*S*]^(*i*)![si99\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si99_e.png) is the best predictor for the source *i*, which has distribution *Q*[*S*]^(*i*)![si100\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si100_e.png). Thus, we find that ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*i*)) ≤ ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*j*)) ∀*j*![si101\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si101_e.png), which implies ∑[*i*]*λ*[*i*]ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*i*)) ≤ ∑[*i*]*λ*[*i*]ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*j*))![si102\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si102_e.png). This further implies that ℒ(*Q*[*T*], *θ*[*T*]) ≤ ℒ(*Q*[*T*], *θ*[*S*]^(*j*)) ∀*j*![si103\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si103_e.png), which in turn concludes the proof $\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{T}) \\leq \\underset{1 \\leq j \\leq n}{\\text{min}}\\;\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{S}^{j})$![si104\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si104_e.png). Finally, suppose the entries of *λ* are strictly positive and let *β* = arg min[*j*]ℒ(*Q*[*T*], *θ*[*S*]^(*j*))![si105\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si105_e.png). Observe that, if there is a source *i* such that the strict inequality ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*i*)) < ℒ(*Q*[*S*]^(*i*), *θ*[*S*]^(*β*))![si106\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si106_e.png) holds, then the main claim of the lemma also becomes strict as we find

$\\begin{array}{r} {\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{T}) \\leq \\sum\\limits\_{i}\\lambda\_{i}\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{S}^{i},\\theta\_{S}^{i}) < \\sum\\limits\_{i}\\lambda\_{i}\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{S}^{i},\\theta\_{S}^{\\beta}) \\leq \\min\\limits\_{j}\\mathcal{L}(Q\_{T},\\theta\_{S}^{j}).} \\\\ \\end{array}$

![si107\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si107_e.png)

Verbally, this strict inequality has a natural meaning that the model *j* is strictly worse than model *i* for the source data *i*.

Observe that the expected loss ℒ![si108\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si108_e.png) defined in [Lemma 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#enun0010) is the supervised loss where one does have the label information. Our proposed target predictor *θ*[*T*] achieves a supervised loss at least as good as the best individual source model. Importantly, the inequality is strict under a natural mild condition: the best individual source model *β* (for the target *Q*[*T*]) is strictly worse than some source model *i* on the source distribution *Q*[*S*]^(*i*)![si100\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si100_e.png).

##  9: Experiments

In this section, we first report and discuss performance of DECISION and subsequently analyze DECISION-mlp.

###  9.1: Experiments on DECISION

####  9.1.1: Datasets

To test the effectiveness of DECISION, experiments on various visual benchmarks of digit and object recognition are done, out of which we only show results on two following object recognition datasets in this chapter.

*   • *Office* ([Hoffman et al., 2018b](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0080)): In this benchmark DA dataset, there are three domains under the office environment namely Amazon (A), DSLR (D), and Webcam (W) with a total of 31 object classes in each domain.
*   • *Office–Home* ([Venkateswara et al., 2017](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0220)): Office–Home consists of four domains, namely, Art(AR), Clipart(CL), Product(PR), and Real-world(RW). Each of these domains contain 65 object classes.

In all of the experiments, turns are taken to fix one of the domains as the target and the rest as the source domains. The source data was discarded after training the source models.

 

####  9.1.2: Baseline methods

Comparison of DECISION is done against a wide array of baselines. Similar to this setting, SHOT ([Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125)) attempts unsupervised adaptation without source data. However, it adapts a single source at a time. Comparison against a multisource extension of SHOT is done via ensembling—by passing the target data through each of the adapted source model and take an average of the soft prediction to obtain the test label. In the comparisons, the name of this method is referred as SHOT-ens. Comparisons against single-source baselines, namely SHOT-best and SHOT-worst, which refer to the best adapted source model and the worst one, respectively, learned using SHOT are also done.

###  9.2: Implementation details

####  9.2.1: Network architecture

For the object recognition tasks, a pretrained ResNet-50 ([He et al., 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0065)) is used as the feature extractor backbone, similar to [Peng et al. (2019)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0165) and [Xu et al. (2019)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0235). Following [Liang et al. (2020)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125) and [Ganin and Lempitsky (2015)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0045), replacement of the penultimate fully connected layer is done with a bottleneck layer and a task-specific classifier layer. Batch normalization ([Ioffe and Szegedy, 2015](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0090)) is utilized after the bottleneck layer, along with weight normalization ([Salimans and Kingma, 2016](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0190)) in the final layer. For the digit recognition task, a variant of the LeNet ([LeCun et al., 1998](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0110)) is used similar to [Liang et al. (2020)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125).

####  9.2.2: Source model training

Following [Liang et al. (2020)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125), training of the source models is done using smooth labels, instead of the usual one-hot encoded labels. This increases the robustness of the model and helps in the adaptation process by encouraging features to lie in tight, evenly separated clusters ([Müller et al., 2019](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0150)). The maximum number of epochs for Office and Office–Home is set to 100 and 50, respectively.

####  9.2.3: Hyper-parameters

The entire framework is trained in an end-to-end fashion via backpropagation. Specifically, stochastic gradient descent is utilized with momentum value 0.9 and weight decay equalling 10^(−3). The learning rate is set at 10^(−2) for the bottleneck and classifier layers, while the backbone is trained at a rate of 10^(−3). In addition, the learning rate scheduling strategy is used from [Ganin and Lempitsky (2015)](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0045), where the initial rate is exponentially decayed as learning progresses. The batch size is set to 32. We use *λ* = 0.3 for all the object recognition tasks and *λ* = 0.1 for the digits benchmark. For adaptation, maximum number of epochs is set to 15, with the pseudo-labels updated at the start of every epoch.

 

###  9.3: Object recognition

####  9.3.1: Office

The results for the three adaptation tasks on the Office dataset are shown in [Table 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0010). DECISION achieves performance at par with the best adapted source models on all the tasks and obtain an average increase of **5.2***%* over SHOT-Ens. In the task of adapting to the Webcam (W) domain, negative transfer from the Amazon (A) model brings the ensemble performance down—this model is able to prevent this, and not only outperforms the ensemble by **3.5***%* but also achieves higher performance than the best adapted source.

[Table 1](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-t0010)

Results on Office.

 
| Source || Method || A,D → W || A,W → D || D,W → A || Avg. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Single || Source-best || 96.3 || 98.4 || 62.5 || 85.7 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  || Source-worst || 75.6 || 80.9 || 62.0 || 72.8 |
|  || SHOT-best ([Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125)) || 98.2 || 99.6 || 75.1 || 90.9 |
|  || SHOT-worst ([Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125)) || 90.6 || 94.2 || 72.9 || 85.9 |
| Multiple || SHOT-Ens ([Liang et al., 2020](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0125)) || 94.9 || 97.8 || 75.0 || 89.3 |
|  || DECISION(Ours) || **98.4** || **99.6** || **75.4** || **91.1** |

A,D, and W are abbreviations of *Amazon*, *DSLR*, and *Webcam*. For single-source methods, Source-best and Source-worst denote the best and worst unadapted source models, whereas SHOT-best and SHOT-worst are the best and worst accuracies of adapted source models.

####  9.3.2: Office–Home

On the Office–Home dataset, the algorithm outperforms all baselines as shown in [Table 2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0015). Across all tasks, this method achieves a mean increase in accuracy of **2***%* over the respective best adapted source models. This can be attributed to the relatively small performance gap between the best and worst adapted sources in comparison to other datasets. This suggests that, as the performance gap between the best and worst performing sources gets smaller, or outlier sources are removed, DECISION can generalize even better to the target.

###  9.4: Ablation study

####  9.4.1: Contribution of each loss

DECISION is trained using a combination of three distinct losses: ℒ[div]![si110\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si110_e.png), ℒ[ent]![si111\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si111_e.png) and ℒ[pl]![si112\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si112_e.png). The contribution of each component of our framework to the adaptation task is shown in [Table 3](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0020). First, both the diversity loss and the pseudo-labeling loss are removed, and trained using only ℒ[ent]![si111\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si111_e.png). Next, ℒ[div]![si110\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si110_e.png) is added in and weighted information maximization is performed. Finally, the results are compared solely using ℒ[pl]![si112\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si112_e.png).

[Table 3](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-t0020)

Loss-wise ablation.

 
| Method || A,D → W || A,W → D || D,W → A || Avg. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| ℒ[*p**l*]![si1\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si1_e.png) || 97.6 || 98.5 || 75.3 || 90.5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  − ℒ[*e**n**t*]![si2\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si2_e.png) || 96.6 || 99.0 || 68.5 || 88.0 |
|  − ℒ[*e**n**t*] + ℒ[*d**i**v*]![si3\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si3_e.png) || 95.9 || 99.0 || 71.6 || 88.9 |
|  − ℒ[*e**n**t*] + ℒ[*d**i**v*] + *λ*ℒ[*p**l*]![si4\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si4_e.png) || **98.4** || **99.6** || **75.4** || **91.1** |

Contribution of each component in adaptation on the Office dataset.

 

####  9.4.2: Analysis on the learned weights

DECISION jointly adapts the source models and learns the weights on each such source. To understand the impact of the weights, the feature extractors are frozen and optimized solely over the weights {*α*[*j*]}[*i* = 1]^(*n*)![si116\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si116_e.png). Naturally, this setup yields better performance compared to trivially assigning equal weights to all source models, as shown in [Table 4](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0025). More interestingly, the learned weights correctly indicate which source model performs better on the target and could serve as a proxy indicator in a model selection framework. See [Fig. 4](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#f0025).

[Table 4](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-t0025)

Performance on freezing backbone network on Office–Home.

 
| Method || AR,CL,PR → RW || AR,CL,RW → PR || AR,PR,RW → CL || CL,PR,RW → AR || Avg. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Source-Ens || 67.6 || 51.4 || 77.7 || 80.1 || 69.2 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DECISION-weights || **68.8** || **52.3** || **79.2** || **80.4** || **70.2** |

DECISION-weight is optimized solely over the source weights and consistently performs better than uniform weighting.

![Fig. 4 Weights as model selection proxy. The weights learnt by DECISION on Office–Home correlates positively with the unadapted source model performance. This essentially means that if an unadapted source model gives good performance on the target, it is more likely to get higher weight compared to those unadapted source models with lesser target accuracy, which is theoretically expected.](images/S0169716122000566/f05-04-9780443184307.jpg)

[Fig. 4](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-f0025) Weights as model selection proxy. The weights learnt by DECISION on Office–Home correlates positively with the unadapted source model performance. This essentially means that if an unadapted source model gives good performance on the target, it is more likely to get higher weight compared to those unadapted source models with lesser target accuracy, which is theoretically expected.

 

####  9.4.3: Distillation into a single model

Since this algorithm is dealing with multiple source models, inference time is of the order 𝒪(*m*)![si117\_e](images/S0169716122000566/si117_e.png), where *m* is the number of source models. If *m* is large, this can lead to inference being quite time consuming. To ameliorate this overhead, a knowledge distillation ([Hinton et al., 2015](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#bb0070)) strategy is followed to obtain a single target model. Teacher supervision is obtained by linearly combining the adapted models via the learned weights. These annotations are subsequently used to train the single student model via vanilla cross-entropy loss. Results obtained using this distillation strategy are shown in [Table 5](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0030). Despite the model compression, the performance remains consistent.

[Table 5](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-t0030)

 Distillation results on object recognition tasks. 

 
| Method  || Office–Home || Office |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RW || PR || CL || AR || A || D || W |
| DECISION (original) || 83.6 || 84.4 || 59.4 || 74.5 || 75.4 || 99.6 || 98.4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DECISION (distillation) || 83.7 || 84.4 || 59.1 || 74.4 || 75.4 || 99.6 || 98.1 |

Performance remains consistent across all datasets despite distilling into a single target model.

###  9.5: Results and analyses of DECISION-mlp

For all the MLPs in this alternative approach ([Algorithm 2](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#b0015)), we set architectures with one/two hidden layers, each with some number of nodes followed by ReLU activation. We show results on Office–Home and Office following the same setup as DECISION. We compare it with DECISION on Office–Home dataset in [Table 6](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0035) and Office in [Table 7](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0040). DECISION-mlp outperforms DECISION for all the targets of Office–Home except for “Product(Pr).” However, in Office dataset we don’t see any improvement over DECISION. For Office–Home two hidden layers with 1024 nodes each are used, whereas for Office one hidden layer with 8192 nodes gives the best performance after an exhaustive grid search. So we check the effect of MLP architectures in [Table 8](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#t0045). We find that the choice of network architecture plays an important role in getting the best possible result, which provides a possible future direction of work.

[Table 6](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-t0035)

Comparison of DECISION and DECISION-mlp on Office–Home.

 
| Method || AR,CL,PR → RW || AR,CL,RW → PR || AR,PR,RW → CL || CL,PR,RW → AR |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DECISION || 83.6 || **84.4** || 59.4 || 74.5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DECISION-mlp || **83.9** || 83 || **60.1** || **74.8** |

[Table 7](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-t0040)

Comparison of DECISION and DECISION-mlp on Office.

 
| Method || A, D → W || A, W → D || D, W → A |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DECISION || **98.4** || **99.6** || **75.4** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DECISION-mlp || 97.6 || **99.6** || 74.6 |

[Table 8](#Ch005_81-105_S0169716122000566.xhtml#B-t0045)

 Ablation of MLP architectures. 

 
| Layers  || Nodes |
| --- | --- |
| 256 || 512 || 1024 || 2048 |
| Single || 73.7 || 74.1 || 74.2 || 74.5 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| Triple || 73.1 || 72.4 || 74 || 73.5 |

We conduct experiments on target “Art” from Office–Home by varying number of nodes and layers of the MLPs. We discover that there is a sweet-spot optimal architecture that gives the best result.

##  10: Conclusions and future work

In this chapter, we discussed the first UDA algorithm that can learn from and optimally combine multiple source models without requiring source data. We analyzed the modification of this algorithm by dropping some assumptions. From the results and ablations of this modified algorithm, we find that there is some potential for the source distribution estimation via MLPs instead of naively assuming them to be uniform. However, the hard part is to search for the optimal MLP architecture for a particular target. A neural architecture search (NAS) kind of approach could be a future direction to search for the optimal MLPs that can efficiently estimate source distributions as accurately as possible. Moreover, there are multiple other exciting directions to pursue. First, we suspect that our algorithm's performance can be further boosted by incorporating data augmentation techniques during training. Second, when there are many source models to utilize, it would be interesting to study whether we can automatically select an optimal subset of the source models without requiring source data in an unsupervised fashion.
